New content
- The chairlift, "the chair that lets go of the rope", a candidate build for ski season (new to these notes; not in
  LINEUP, and no lift among the made builds): the first ones ran in December 1936, so December 2026 makes 90 years.
  Checked facts: a railroad engineer, James Curran, worked from "conveyor belts used to load bananas onto ships in
  Central America" (Smithsonian) and "replaced the hooks on which bananas hung with chairs" (Union Pacific, Dec 3 2019).
  The lifts on Proctor and Dollar mountains at Sun Valley, Idaho "opened with the resort in December 1936" as "single
  chairs that were affixed to an overhead cable" (Smithsonian), loading at "between four to five miles per hour", and
  "A lift chair was attached to the side of a truck for the test", with roller skates for skis (UP). Before them, rope
  tows and T-bars were "uncomfortable and awkward, and on occasion, dangerous" (Linda Hall Library, Jun 9 2022); one
  1936 lift was "later replaced and moved to Rudd Mountain" and "has not operated since 1966" (Linda Hall). Today's fast
  lift has a "spring-loaded cable grip which detaches at terminals", runs "averaging 1,000 feet per minute" against "a
  typical fixed-grip speed of 500 feet per minute", and crawls through the station at "a typical speed of 200 feet per
  minute" (Wikipedia, Detachable chairlift) on "rubber tires that guide chairs through the base terminal" (Colorado Ski
  History). "Tension is provided either by a counterweight system or by hydraulic or pneumatic rams", for "variations
  in rope length due to temperature" and to "maintain friction between the rope and the drive bullwheel"; "an
  anti-rollback device (usually a cam) also acts on the bullwheel"; load tests use "bags of water" (Wikipedia,
  Chairlift); "Insufficient grip force triggers an alarm and brings the lift to a halt" (a maker's line quoted by
  Wikipedia). A chain (breaks marked "ours" are ours): A1, green banana bunches swing on hooks along a moving cable into
  a ship's hold at a tropical port, then a hard cut to the same hooks carrying single wooden chairs up a snowy 1930s
  mountain. A2, 1930s skiers seen from behind trudging up a steep slope in deep snow while a rope tow drags others,
  wide. A3, in a rail yard a chair hanging from a tall wooden rig on the side of a moving truck scoops up a rider on
  roller skates, seen from behind. A4 (ours), the chair never slows: at the bottom station it bumps a skier seen from
  behind into the seat, poles flying. A5, macro cutaway of a station: a spring grip opens on a rail, the chair rolls
  off the cable onto a row of spinning tires that slow it to walking pace, then speed it up, and the grip snaps shut on
  the cable. A6 (ours), on a frozen night the long steel cable sags between the towers and slips on the big drive
  wheel. B1, a giant concrete counterweight hanging in a tower pulls the wheel back and the cable runs taut again. B2
  (ours), the motor stops with every uphill chair loaded and the cable starts creeping backwards. B3, a cam bites the
  big wheel and the line stops dead, then a warning light flags a grip that holds too weakly. B4, a tease of whichever
  build follows. B5, a load test: bags of water strapped to every uphill chair of a new six-seat lift, inspectors
  watching from behind, then opening-day crowds from behind riding up. Line 12: "1,000 FT/MIN" (or "200" in the
  station). Line 13: ninety years after the banana chairs, the chair lets go of the rope at every station and grabs it
  again. B6: funny slow motion, a mitten slips from a rider's hand on a chairlift, tumbles down and lands on a snowman's
  head. Check before a line uses them: Wikipedia's first high-speed detachable quad (Breckenridge, 1981) is marked
  "citation needed", its Chairlift page contradicts the detachable page on loading speeds, and the Sun Valley lift
  lengths differ between Smithsonian and Linda Hall. No makers' names on screen and no real people's faces (Curran
  and the resort's founder in words only, if at all). Free video ends Nov 28, so if the main chat wants this build its
  pair has to run by then.
  [Smithsonian](https://www.smithsonianmag.com/innovation/how-railroad-engineer-from-nebraska-invented-worlds-first-ski-chairlift-180976878/),
  [Union Pacific, Dec 3 2019](https://www.up.com/news/heritage/tr071719-ski-lift),
  [Linda Hall Library, Jun 9 2022](https://www.lindahall.org/about/news/scientist-of-the-day/james-curran),

## 2026-11-25 12:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Trends
- The world's largest wildlife crossing opens on Wed Dec 2, a news peg and a candidate build (new to these notes; not
  in LINEUP, and no animal bridge among the made builds): the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing over the US 101 at
  Liberty Canyon, Agoura Hills, near Los Angeles, with "the official Dec. 2 opening ceremony" (AP via ADN, Nov 18
  2026). It is "strictly for animal use", with "a public viewing platform" (Fox LA, Apr 25 2026), and "a lone coyote
  believed to be the first mammal to make the trek safely across" has already used it (ADN). Checked facts: it
  "stretches 1,250 feet in two sections over the roadways" (ADN), "Spanning 10 lanes of the 101 Freeway" (Fox LA);
  the freeway span is "210-ft-long, 174-ft-wide", built from "82 precast box girders weighing 60 tons each", trucked in
  "three to four of the units each night, requiring only half of the freeway to be closed at a time", over "43
  consecutive nights of alternating nightly closures" with a "500-ton crane" (ENR, Aug 19 2025); on top, "up to 4 ft
  of soil and 5,000 plants native to the Santa Monica Mountains", "6,000 cu yd of soil" designed "from the molecular
  level on up" (ENR), from "over 1.1 million hyper-local seeds" collected by hand (Fox LA); "10-foot sound-dampening
  walls that line the span" and "Fencing along the freeway funnels animals to the crossing" (ADN); lighting "chosen to
  minimize impact to wildlife" and concrete pigmented against "sky glow" (ENR). Why: lions boxed in by freeways "risk
  inbreeding themselves into extinction", and "at least 780 mountain lions — or 18% of the state's population — are
  killed by vehicles each year" (ADN). Work "began on Earth Day 2022" (Fox LA). Cost: "$114 million total budget",
  up from "an initial $92 million" (ADN; ENR's "$92-million" is the older figure). A chain (breaks marked "ours" are
  ours): A1, at dusk a mountain lion walks calmly through tall grass on a green bridge while ten lanes of headlights
  stream underneath; A2, wide aerial of the freeway cutting the mountains in two, a lion stopping at the edge of the
  roaring road; A3, at night a giant crane lowers precast girders across half the freeway while traffic runs on the
  other half; A4 (ours), on the bare concrete deck a deer sniffs and turns back; A5, feet of soil spread and native
  shrubs and oaks planted, seeds sown (hands only); A6 (ours), headlight glare and roaring traffic noise wash over
  the young plants and a coyote flinches back; B1, the 10-foot walls rise along both edges and the bridge goes dark
  and quiet; B2 (ours), a bobcat ignores the bridge and slips down to the open road a mile away; B3, a long fence
  along the freeway leads it to the crossing; B4, a tease of whichever build follows; B5, the real crossing on a
  night trail camera, a coyote trotting across (as in ADN); B6, funny slow motion, a squirrel leaps for a bird feeder
  and swings round it. Line 12: "10 LANES" or "780" (lions killed on California roads a year). Check before use: the
  height over the road and the opening-day program were on none of these pages; the donor's name in words only, no
  real people's faces; P-22, the lion "euthanized in 2022 after likely being hit by a car" (ADN), at most one gentle
  line. Free video ends Nov 28, so if the main chat wants this build its pair has to run by then.

  (ATR)" and goes through "Explosive Detection System (EDS) machines" (Wikipedia); a flagged bag is "diverted to
  manual screening stations where security officers physically examine the contents", tilt trays run "at up to 30

  (Jun 12 2025, 2024 data): "The mishandling rate dropped to 6.3 bags per 1,000 passengers in 2024, down from 6.9 the
  previous year", "33.4 million" bags, about "$5 billion", transfers "the biggest contributor at 41%", and IATA
  Resolution 753 "mandates baggage tracking at four key stages". The famous break, Denver (GAO, Oct 14 1994): "over
  17 miles of track; 5.5 miles of conveyors; 4,000 telecarts", "up to 70 bags per minute" at "speeds of up to 24 miles
  per hour"; in tests "bags were misloaded, were misrouted, or fell out of telecarts, causing the system to jam" and it
  "loaded bags into telecarts that were already full"; the airport, due "in October 1993", lost "between $18 million

Free footage and pictures
- ESO, ESA/Hubble and ESA/Webb give real space and telescope footage under CC BY 4.0, commercial use included (new
  to these notes: the Nov 24 08:10 entry has ESA's own share-alike licence, which these three sites do not use). ESO:
  "the images, videos, and music distributed on the public ESO website ... are licensed under a Creative Commons
  Attribution 4.0 International License" and "may on a non-exclusive basis be reproduced without fee provided the
  credit is clear and visible"; ESA/Hubble and ESA/Webb: "images, videos and web texts are released under the Creative
  Commons Attribution 4.0 International license". The rules that matter for us: the credit ("ESO", "ESA/Hubble",
  "ESA/Webb", or the item's own credit line) "with the wording unaltered"; for video a credit in the description is
  not enough: it goes "burned in" or overlaid, or "clearly visible as a credit at the end"; ESO adds "you must clearly
  indicate if you have used AI to modify our content"; no logos, no implied endorsement, no identifiable people in
  commercial use, and music is out (ESO's Music Archive aside). Files are big: ESO's "An update on the world's biggest
  eye on the sky" (Jul 20 2026, 1 min 58 s) comes as "4K Ultra HD Preview H.264" (426.8 MB) or "Full HD Preview
  1080p" (248.8 MB). Fetch media takes only cloudfront.net and higgsfield.ai links up to 300 MB, so using these needs
  ESO's download host added to .github/workflows/fetch-media.yml (the 1080p file fits the size cap; the main chat's
  call) or a download in Chrome.
  [ESO, usage of images and videos](https://elt.eso.org/public/copyright/),
  [ESA/Hubble, usage](https://esahubble.org/copyright/), [ESA/Webb, copyright](https://esawebb.org/copyright),
  [ESO video potw2629a, Jul 20 2026](https://eso.org/public/videos/potw2629a/)

New content
- The Extremely Large Telescope, a candidate build whose B5 can be ESO's own footage after Nov 28 (new to these notes;
  no telescope in LINEUP or among the made builds). Checked: the main mirror will be "39 metres in diameter and
  consist of 798 segments"; M4 is "the largest adaptive mirror ever built, with a diameter of 2.4 metres"; the site is
  Cerro Armazones "at an altitude of 3060 metres"; first test observations "in 2029", science "expected to be
  delivered in December 2030" (ESO timeline; the 2023 midpoint report still had 2028); "the dome reached its highest
  point of 80 meters" in April 2025, the main structure is "currently weighing around 3500 tonnes" and has "recently
  rotated around its vertical axis" for the first time, and "The entire project is more than 75% complete" (ESO video,
  Jul 20 2026); the dome's upper section "will rotate on the concrete 'pier' below", with shock absorbers at its base
  against earthquakes (Space.com, Feb 16 2026). A chain (breaks marked "ours" are ours): A1, night on a bare desert
  mountain, a giant metal dome opens and a mirror of hundreds of hexagons catches the Milky Way, slow push-in; A2, a
  star in a small telescope shimmers and smears; A3, hexagons set edge to edge into a 39 m honeycomb; A4 (ours), the
  cold night and the wind tilt a few segments and the star smears into copies; A5, sensors and small pistons nudge
  them back into line (check before use); A6, an earthquake shakes the mountain; B1, the dome rides it out on its
  shock absorbers; B2, the air itself makes the star twinkle; B3, a thin 2.4 m mirror flexes to undo the twinkle and
  the star snaps sharp; B4, a tease of whichever build follows; B5, ESO's real dome and structure (its clips, credited
  at the end); B6, funny slow motion, someone zooms a phone at the full moon and gets a white blob. Line 12: "798" or
  "39 M". Check before use: the segment size, M4's thickness, actuators and how often it corrects, the laser guide
  stars, the dome's width and mass and the cost (the timeline gives only a 2006 study budget) were on none of these
  pages. Free Seedance ends Nov 28: if the main chat wants this build later, ESO's CC BY clips and pictures can carry
  B5 and lines 12-13.
  [ESO, ELT timeline](https://elt.eso.org/about/timeline/),
  [ESO video potw2629a](https://eso.org/public/videos/potw2629a/),

New content
- SLIM, JAXA's "Moon Sniper", as a candidate build (new to these notes; no moon lander in LINEUP or among the made
  builds). Its hook picture is real and strange: a lander standing on its nose on the Moon, photographed by a
  baseball-sized rover. Checked: launched 7 Sep 2023 on an H-IIA with XRISM; the goal was "within 100 m of the target
  site"; it touched down on Jan 19 2024 (Jan 20 in Japan) "about 55 m east of the original landing site", "under 10 m
  of the landing site chosen by the craft's real-time navigation system" (Physics World; JAXA's Sakai: "possibly
  within 3 to 4 meters", Space.com); one of its two main engines lost thrust "about 50 meters" up (PBS) and a camera
  "had captured an engine nozzle lying on the lunar surface" (Space.com); it ended "rotated at 90 degrees, effectively
  landing on the tip of its nose", its solar panels facing west, so no power, and it "powered down on the surface, as
  planned, to avoid over-discharge" (Space.com, PBS, TechCrunch); the landing was "vision-based", with "an onboard
  laser-range finder, a camera and a radar"; LEV-1 ("2.1 kg") hops; LEV-2, the "baseball-sized" SORA-Q built by JAXA
  with Sony, toymaker Tomy and Doshisha University, took the upside-down photo and sent it home through LEV-1 (Physics
  World; PBS: "275 images" between the two); Japan became "only the fifth country to make a soft landing on the moon".
  Its nights, checked too: it "briefly powered back on nine days later, when the direction of incoming sunlight
  changed enough to power SLIM's solar cells" (Live Science); a lunar night is "the roughly 14-day period" without
  sunlight (Live Science), with temperatures "as low as minus 274 degrees Fahrenheit (minus 170 degrees Celsius)"
  (Space.com); it "wasn't designed to withstand even one" such night, yet it answered on Feb 25, again in mid-March and
  on Apr 23 2024 (JAXA: "SLIM has maintained its primary functions even after three overnight stays, which was not
  anticipated in its design"; Space.com, Apr 24 2024).
  A chain (breaks marked "ours" are ours): A1, a box-shaped lander stands on its nose on a grey crater slope, a tiny
  ball rover rolls up and looks at it, slow push-in; A2 (ours), landers that come down anywhere in a wide zone, far
  from the rock they came for; A3, the lander's camera compares the ground below with its map (check before use) and
  steers to one exact spot; A4, 50 m up one engine nozzle breaks off and tumbles away; A5, the other engine still sets
  it down within 10 m of its pick; A6, it rolls onto its nose, panels facing away from the sun, its lights die; B1, it
  sleeps to save its battery, and days later the sunlight comes round onto its panels and it wakes; B2, the lunar night
  falls and the lander sits alone in total darkness under the stars for two weeks, time-lapse; B3, sunrise creeps back
  across the crater and the lander's antenna sends a faint signal toward the blue Earth; B4, a tease of whichever build
  follows; B5, a baseball-sized ball splits open on the dust and photographs the upside-down lander, then a control room
  watches that picture arrive, people from behind (JAXA's own photo is out, see above); B6, funny slow motion, a slice
  of buttered toast slides off a plate and lands butter side down. Line 12: "55 M" or "3 NIGHTS". Check before use:
  the matching method, how SORA-Q moves, SLIM's size and mass, when contact ended, and the landing zones of earlier
  missions (none on these pages). No real faces.
  [Space.com](https://www.space.com/japan-slim-moon-precise-landing),
  [Space.com, Apr 24 2024](https://www.space.com/japan-slim-moon-lander-survives-3rd-lunar-night),
  [Live Science](https://www.livescience.com/space/the-moon/slim-lives-japans-upside-down-moon-lander-survives-freezing-lunar-night-defying-all-expectations),
  [Physics World](https://physicsworld.com/a/japans-lunar-lander-falls-head-over-heels-for-the-moon/),
  [PBS NewsHour](https://www.pbs.org/newshour/science/japans-1st-moon-lander-has-hit-its-target-but-it-appears-to-be-upside-down),
  [TechCrunch, Jan 25 2024](https://techcrunch.com/2024/01/25/japans-slim-spacecraft-sticks-moon-landing-upside-down/)

  (the breaks are ours): A1, dusk, a lone cart rolls across an empty parking lot to a painted line at its edge, its
  front wheel locks and the cart jerks, skids and swings sideways, slow motion. A2, a 1930s grocery, shoppers' hands
  straining under heavy wire hand baskets. A3, Sylvan Goldman's 1936 prototype "consisted of two folding chairs placed
  seat-to-seat with wheels attached to the bottom and a basket on top"; the cart came out "on June 4, 1937, at the
  Humpty Dumpty grocery store in Oklahoma City" (Smithsonian), built as "a metal frame that held two wire baskets"
  (Wikipedia). A4, nobody pushed it: men "considered the carts emasculating and said trundling them around was
  'women's work'" and it sat "unloved and unused at the front of his store" (Smithsonian); "women found them
  suggestive of a baby carriage" (Wikipedia). A5, "He hired attractive models to shop in the store while confidently
  pushing the carts" (Smithsonian), people from behind; "By 1940, store owners looking to buy new shopping carts
  reportedly faced a seven-year wait list". A6, rows of carts fill the front of the store and block the doors (ours).
  B1, the nesting cart: "In 1946, Orla Watson devised a system for a telescoping (i.e., "nesting") shopping cart",
  "patent #2,479,530 on August 16, 1949", fitting "into another cart for compact storage via a swinging one-way rear
  door" (Wikipedia); the Smithsonian has Goldman making "the more familiar nesting cart (following a patent war over
  the design)": the two pages' years differ, so check before a line uses one. B2, carts far from the store (a creek, a
  bus stop, a field): they "typically cost between $75 and $150 each, with some models costing $300–400", theft
  "estimated at $800 million worldwide per annum" (Wikipedia, no year: check), "every cart saved is $150 you don't
  spend" (Willamette Week). B3, the lock: in 1968 Elmer Isaacks' patent had "a magnetic mechanism [that] would push a
  rod through a hole in the wheel"; now "Today's perimeter is just a buried wire carrying electric pulses", "An
  induction coil in the wheel picks up these pulses and passes them to an onboard computer", "activating an internal,
  motor-driven braking system", and "the lock is armed, and will engage if it goes out the door without first going
  through a checkout line" (Willamette Week, May 15 2022); "Store personnel must then deactivate the lock with a
  handheld remote control" (Wikipedia). The coin lock is a second fix if a line needs one: "the user inserts a coin,
  token or card to unlock the cart, which is returned when they return it" (Wikipedia). B4, the next LINEUP build. B5,
  a packed parking lot at dawn on Black Friday, streams of full carts rolling to the cars, wide aerial, people from
  behind. B6, funny slow motion, one front wheel flutters wildly and the cart veers into a pyramid of oranges that
  rolls across the aisle. Line 12: "1937" or "20-25 MILLION" (the source gives a range). Leave out the hacking talks on triggering the locks (and
  their signal details), store and brand names in shots, and Goldman's face (hands and backs only). Timing as for New
  Year's Eve: Seedance is free only until Nov 28 and museum-A/B and rainvortex-A/B go first, so adding this build is

  flight" (CGTN, May 25 2026). How it works: plain GPS is metres off, so a base station on the ground sends RTK
  corrections that take each drone "from multiple meters to just a couple centimeters", GPS time ("around 100
  billionths of a second") starts them together, and each drone is "constantly attempting to hit a moving target" on
  its own flight plan; a pilot "commands" the show and "Computers execute a show" (Verge Aero). The paths are loaded

  each drone "communicates and shares data with its neighbours": the sources disagree, so a line says only that every

- The revolving door, a winter candidate (new to these notes; not in LINEUP, where the Kuwait Towers' turning deck is
  another thing). Theophilus Van Kannel of Philadelphia patented a "Storm-door structure" (US 387,571, Aug 7 1888)
  after a German "Tür ohne Luftzug" (a door without draught, 1881), and the first one turned at Rector's restaurant in
  Times Square in 1899 (Wikipedia; 99% Invisible). The patent says it "effectually prevents the entrance of wind,
  snow, rain or dust" and is "perfectly noiseless" (Wikipedia); the motto is "always open, always closed" (99%
  Invisible). The physics: "In the winter, heated air rises toward the top of a building" and cold air is drawn in at the ground
  floor, and "revolving doors also counter the effects of stack pressure, which increases in taller buildings" (Boon
  Edam's course), while "When a swing door is opened at ground level, this causes an in-rush of air" (Hackaday, Jun 9
  2022). The break: at MIT's building E25 in 2006 "just 23 percent of visitors used the revolving doors" (Boon Edam;
  99% Invisible says about 25, Mental Floss 20-30: say "about one in four"), and with everyone using them "MIT would
  save almost $7,500 in natural gas a year" and "nearly 15 tons of CO2" (MIT Tech Talk, quoted by Boon Edam); "There's
  8 times more air exchanged when you use the swing door than when you use the revolving door" (99% Invisible: say it
  that way round, not "eight times less"). At Columbia, signs took use from 28% to 71% (99% Invisible: a small
  informal test). Safety: the doors "typically have a "speed control" (governor) to prevent people from spinning the
  doors too fast", the "bookfold" design "allows all three or four wings to be broken away together" (Wikipedia), and
  "many building codes mandate that swinging doors also be present" (Hackaday). For line 14: "Compartments terrify
  people" (99% Invisible). A chain (the breaks are ours): A1, a four-wing door turning slowly in a blizzard, snow
  whirling outside, a calm warm lobby inside; A2, a tower in winter, warm air rising up its core and a gale sucked in
  through the lobby's swing doors; A3, the wings that never leave a straight path for the air; A4, people walking past
  it to the swing door; A5, a sign by the door, more people push the wings; A6, someone spins it far too fast; B1, the
  governor; B2, a queue of prams and trolleys piling up at the turning door; B3, the wings folding flat like a book;
  B5, a hotel door at rush hour in the snow, people from behind; B6, funny slow motion, a man and a huge suitcase
  jammed in one compartment. Line 12: "1888" or "8 TIMES". Leave out the chivalry story (an urban legend: "according to
  Snopes, there is no evidence to support this"), the 1942 Boston nightclub fire (the folding wings can be told without
  it), the ANSI figures (only in a summary of the course) and the vendor's "eight times as energy efficient". A Gulf
  line (the same door keeping the cool air in) needs its own source.
  [Wikipedia, Revolving door](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolving_door),
  [99% Invisible](https://99percentinvisible.org/episode/revolving-doors/transcript/),

  longest roller coaster" (Coaster101; none of the five pages read names Guinness). Numbers: "250 km/h" (Wikipedia,
  Intamin); "4,250 m" of track (Wikipedia; "13,943 feet", Coaster101); an "elevation change of approximately 195 m"
  because it "uses the natural cliffs near the park" (Wikipedia; Intamin: "the top of the 195 meters cliff"), while its
  tallest hill is 163 m (Wikipedia) or "a 165-meter camelback" (Intamin), so the 195 m is the cliff, not a tower: check
  before a line gives a hill height; about three and a half minutes ("approximately 3 minutes 25 seconds", Intamin;
  3:35 in Wikipedia's box). The speed comes with no chain at all: "three linear synchronous motor (LSM) launches"
  (Wikipedia), the first a slow climb, "an LSM lift hill, with a constant speed of 40 km/h" (Intamin), over "more than
  700 LSM modules" (Wikipedia; Intamin: "more than 722 LSM stators"); the second carries the train "up the Tuwaiq
  cliffs" (160 km/h in Wikipedia, "a 150 km/h LSM launch" in Intamin: check), where it is "slowed down by brakes at
  the edge of the cliff before dropping at an angle of 90 degrees into a tunnel" (Wikipedia); the third is "the third,
  final, and fastest launch" (Intamin). The desert: "protecting riders from airborne sand at high speeds" (Wikipedia)
  with "curved windshields at the front of each car" (Wikipedia) that end "the need for goggles", "windshields and
  spoilers to protect passengers' eyes and ears" (Intamin); "large wheels with ventilated rims" (Intamin; "16 inches
  (41 cm) in diameter", Wikipedia) and a chassis "fully machined with no welding involved" (Wikipedia; Intamin:
  "weld-free design"), for "the Saudi summer heat" (Intamin). Announced in 2019 "with the park slated to open in early
  2023" (Wikipedia); six trains of 14 riders, at least 130 cm tall (Wikipedia). A chain (breaks marked "ours" are
  ours): A1 at sunset a train races up a sheer desert cliff, tips over the edge and drops straight down into a tunnel
  in the rock, aerial; A2 a flat plateau ending at a cliff about 200 m high, heat haze, a tiny chain lift crawling up a
  giant steel tower beside it (ours: the slow way up); A3 the track laid up the cliff face itself; A4 a train crawling
  up the cliff in the blazing sun, far too slowly (ours); A5 cutaway of rows of electromagnets along the track pulling
  a magnet fin under the car, the train blasting up the slope; A6 at full speed a gust of sand sweeps across the track
  toward the riders' hands on the lap bars (ours); B1 curved windshields at the front of each car, the sand streaming
  past them; B2 wheels spinning at full speed glow hot and a welded joint trembles (ours); B3 wide wheels with vented
  rims on a one-piece machined steel chassis, cool; B4 the next LINEUP build; B5 the real ride on a clear evening, the
  train dropping 90 degrees into the tunnel, small figures watching far below; B6 funny slow motion, a grown man
  folded into a tiny kiddie coaster at a fair, knees at his chin, gripping the bar as it crawls along. Line 12: "250
  KM/H". Riders only from behind or small; no park logo or branding. Its Seedance pair could only run by Nov 28 (in
  practice the v2 look), and adding it is the main chat's call.

## 2026-11-25 23:05 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- The automatic rice cooker, a candidate everyday build for both channels (new to these notes; no rice build in LINEUP
  or kit/series; its cousins the pressure cooker, 76, and the kettle, 86, work differently: this one never reads a
  clock, it waits for the water to run out). The first: "Toshiba's ER-4, introduced in 1955, was the world's first
  automatic rice cooker", "on sale on 10 December 1955" (IEEE Spectrum; Wikipedia gives the same date, "or 1956").
  "At its launch, 700 units were produced, but they did not sell well" (Wikipedia); then "Within a year, Toshiba was
  selling more than 200,000 rice cookers a month" (IEEE), and "By 1960" it was "in use in about half of all Japanese
  households" (Wikipedia), at 3,200 yen, "about a third of the average Japanese monthly salary" (IEEE; Wikipedia: a
  graduate's starting pay). The problem it ended: "getting up by 5:00 am to fan the flames of a kamado", rice made "up
  to three times a day" (IEEE); the electric pots before it "still needed the heat to be turned off manually"
  (Wikipedia, "Fumiko Minami"). The 1955 trick: a double pot, "filling the outer pot with a glass of water and heating
  it" (Wikipedia); "One cup of boiling water in the external pot took 20 minutes to evaporate", then "a bimetallic
  switch" in the outer pot "would bend and cut the circuit" (IEEE), so it "cooked the rice for exactly 20 minutes and
  then turned itself off" (IEEE caption). How a basic cooker knows today: the water "stays at boiling point (100 °C,
  212 °F)" while it lasts, and "When the water has all been absorbed, the temperature can rise above boiling point,
  which trips the thermostat" (Wikipedia). In simple cookers that thermostat is a magnet and a small button of "an
  alloy that has a Curie temperature just a bit higher than the boiling point of water": past that point "the magnet
  is no longer attracted to it, so the spring overcomes the magnet and… *click*" it drops to warming (kottke, Jan 12
  2024, a quoted passage; a Medium post agrees: it "loses its magnetism at temperatures above 100° C"). No maker's
  page was read and none gives a number, so say "just past 100 degrees", not "103". Later steps: warming at
  "approximately 65 °C (150 °F)"; "In 1972, a rice cooker with a heat-retention function", a microcomputer in 1979,
  induction heating in 1988 (Wikipedia). People: Wikipedia credits "Toshiba's Shogo Yamada beginning in 1951" and "a
  breakthrough invention by Yoshitada Minami", tested by his wife Fumiko "on her rooftop in cold winters and scorching
  summers" (IEEE), but Toshiba's museum page names no inventor and IEEE's writer found no mention of Minami before a
  2012 book chapter: the narration says "Toshiba, 1955". A chain (breaks marked "ours" are ours): A1 cutaway of a
  white rice cooker, the last water gone from the pot, a small magnet under it lets go with a click and the red light
  turns amber, slow push-in; A2 before dawn in a 1950s farmhouse kitchen, a cook seen from behind fans the fire of a
  clay stove under an iron rice pot, smoke in the first light, wide; A3 an early electric pot of rice boiling on a
  kitchen table, plugged into the wall; A4 nobody switches it off and the bottom layer scorches black, smoke curling up
  (ours); A5 cutaway of a double pot, a glass of water poured into the outer pot and slowly boiling away around the
  inner pot of rice; A6 a test pot on a frosty rooftop in a winter wind, the cold slowing it down (ours: the weather
  changes the time); B1 macro of a strip of two metals bending in the heat and snapping a switch open; B2 the rice
  done at noon, cold and hard by dinner (ours); B3 a low warming glow under the pot, a wisp of steam for hours; B4 the
  next LINEUP build; B5 a 1950s department store counter stacked with boxed white rice cookers, shoppers from behind,
  wide; B6 funny slow motion, a man lifts the lid to check for the tenth time and a puff of steam fogs his glasses
  while the cat watches. Line 12: "200,000 A MONTH". Keep the steam soft ("a wisp of steam"): "a jet of white steam"
  came back NSFW on Nov 23 (HIGGSFIELD.md rule 3). No brand or logo on the cookers; for Kef Tenbeni the tag can end on
  a big family tray of rice. Its Seedance pair could only run by Nov 28, so in practice the v2 look; adding it is the
  main chat's call.
  [IEEE Spectrum](https://spectrum.ieee.org/toshiba-rice-cooker), [Wikipedia, Rice cooker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_cooker),
  [Wikipedia, Fumiko Minami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumiko_Minami),
  [Toshiba Science Museum](https://toshiba-mirai-kagakukan.jp/a-guide/en/history-zone/ichigoki/cooker.htm),
  [kottke, Jan 12 2024](https://also.kottke.org/24/01/the-clever-engineering-trick-that-allows-simple-rice-cookers-to-perfectly-cook-rice),
  [Medium, 0xmachina](https://medium.com/0xmachina/cooking-with-magnetism-for-real-f4ff8b1dc51a)

## 2026-11-26 04:02 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

New content
- The Kariba Dam on the Zambezi (Zambia and Zimbabwe), a candidate build from Africa whose repair is due to finish this
  year (new to these notes; not in LINEUP). Hook: the water it lets out dug a hole at its own feet. The spillway jets
  "progressively scoured a deep and steep-sided plunge pool hole with a current bottom at about 70 m below the original
  riverbed and about 80 m below the normal tailwater level" (EPFL model study, 2010); Mining Weekly (May 9 2014): "from
  an initial 10 m when the dam was built in 1959 to nearly 90 m", "gradually moving towards the 128-m-high wall" (the
  two depths disagree: say "about 70 m", from EPFL). Break: in 2014 engineers warned that a collapse "would put the
  lives and livelihoods of more than 3.5-million people in Zimbabwe, Zambia and Mozambique at risk" (Mining Weekly; the
  river authority said "This does not translate into an immediate calamity"). Second break: the concrete swells
  (alkali-aggregate reaction), which made the six sluice gates "more difficult to open and close smoothly". Fixes, per
  the Zambezi River Authority (May 7 2026): the plunge pool reshaping, "completed and commissioned in September 2024";
  hydro-demolition and concrete repairs upstream of the gates (99%); new hoists for all six gates (about 70%, "expected
  to be fully completed by September 2026"); the whole project "approximately 94% complete", "on course for completion
  by the end of the fourth Quarter of 2026", about US$294 million. Numbers (World Bank fact sheet, Feb 24 2015): a 128 m
  double-curvature concrete arch, 617 m crest, commissioned 1960, "181 billion cubic meters of water" ("the largest
  man-made reservoir in the world"), 1,830 MW, "more than 10,035 GWh" a year. A1 picture: the six gates open, white
  jets plunging into a deep rock hole at the foot of the curved wall. Open before a script: no page after May 2026 says
  whether the works are finished (the finish would be the B5 news peg); the rock volume, the cofferdam and the 1957-58
  construction floods were not on the pages read (Wikipedia did not load). Keep to the engineering (no resettlement or
  river-legend angle: the politics and religion rules).
  [EPFL, 2010](https://www.epfl.ch/labs/lch/wp-content/uploads/2018/11/Hydraulic-Modelling-of-the-Kariba-Dam-Plunge.-Determination-of-the-optimum-pool-profile.pdf),

  laterally": a fixed grip. So the first chairs never let go of the rope, and the detachable grip of the build is the
  later fix (the "before and after" is our reading). No copyright notice in the page text (OCR text read: check the

- Bubble wrap (LINEUP 103, queued as bubblewrap-A/B), its patent drawing as a free proof still (new to these notes;
  the Nov 24 21:05 entry had only the Smithsonian caption's "U.S. Pat. No. 3,142,599"; the USPTO rule on patent

  27 1959 and granted July 28 1964, names one inventor, "Marc A. Chavannes", assigned to "Sealed Air Corp." (so the
  1957 story keeps both men, and the patent is Chavannes' alone). Three sheets, FIGS. 1-23: FIG. 1 the molding-roller
  machine, FIGS. 6-7 an exploded and a plan view of the sheet, whose cells are hexagons, FIGS. 8-9 the roller, and
  variants with round and dome cells, paper or fabric backing and a foil barrier. How it is made, for B3 or a proof
  line: "the heated plastic film is drawn into these openings by vacuum means including tubes 22 connected with a
  manifold 23", the second film is fused on by "heating at least the surfaces of the layers to a fusing temperature
  and then urging the heated surfaces into contact" (it "does not employ adhesives"), leaving "sealed pockets in which
  air or other fluid is entrapped", for "packaging of products, padding of furniture, cars and trucks, heat
  insulation and the like". His follow-up, US 3,294,387 "Laminated cellular material" (published Dec 27 1966, no
  assignee), claims "entrapping a compressible gas in said embossments" (read through a fetch summary). No copyright
  notice on either page. Build-It use: FIG. 1 or FIGS. 6-7 as a framed insert with a slow zoom for 103's lines 12-13.

- The pin tumbler lock (LINEUP 111), father and son (new to these notes; the 12:10 entry left open which patent the
  cylinder lock is credited to): the father came first, "June 13, 1844 ... pin tumbler safe lock — Springfield,
  Massachusetts" (Wikipedia, Linus Yale Sr., patent list), and Wikipedia's "Pin tumbler lock" says "In 1848, Linus
  Yale Sr. invented the modern pin-tumbler lock". The son made it small: "In 1861, Linus Yale Jr. ... invented and
  patented a smaller flat key with serrated edges" (same page), "Yale patented his cylinder pin-tumbler lock in 1861"
  (Lemelson-MIT), while his own Wikipedia page gives a bank lock in 1851, "his pin tumbler lock for use in doors in
  1863" and the June 27 1865 patent for "Cylinder locks and other locks with tumbler pins" (US 48,475, the 12:10
  entry's drawing); ASME says 1861 in its summary and 1865 in its text. So the script says "a father and son" and
  "the 1860s", never one exact year. Beats for the breaks: Yale Jr. "did live demonstrations to corporate business
  executives and government officials that showed how he successfully picked the locks that were in operation"
  (Wikipedia), and in 1868 he and Henry R. Towne founded the Yale Lock Manufacturing Company in Stamford, Connecticut
  (Wikipedia, Lemelson); he died that Christmas (the 24th or 25th: the pages differ). The old lock: "around 4,000
  years ago, in locks that were very large (up to two feet long) and made out of wood" (Lemelson), but Wikipedia's pin
  tumbler page puts the origin "between 2500 and 1800 BCE in Mesopotamia" and calls Egypt the usually cited one: say
  "about 4,000 years ago" and "giant wooden locks", not "invented in Egypt". Pins: "typically five or six".
  No free picture of an old Egyptian lock: the Science Museum Group's "Ancient Egyptian lock" (1912-294, 620 mm tall)
  is "modern Egyptian construction" of about 1912, its sectioned "Working model of Egyptian pin lock" (1966-364, from
  Chubb) is "CC BY-NC-SA 4.0" (commercial use is licensed through the Science and Society Picture Library), a 1970
  Faroe Islands replica (1970-391) is "© The Board of Trustees of the Science Museum", and the Global Egyptian
  Museum's Roman-period wooden key (National Museum of Ireland 1914:221, "with two tangs") shows no rights line. So
  111's ancient lock stays a Seedance or picture-model shot, and a museum "Egyptian lock" is often a modern replica:
  read the record before calling one ancient.

## 2026-11-26 14:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Free footage and pictures
- Public Domain Day 2027, five weeks out (new to these notes; it bears on footage for after Nov 28): on Jan 1 2027
  every work first published in the US in 1931 joins the US public domain (renewed works of that era run 95 years
  from publication, 17 U.S.C. § 304(b), and every term ends on Dec 31 of its last year, § 305; unrenewed 1931 works
  were free already), and so do sound recordings first published in 1926 (the 100-year step for 1923-1946
  recordings, § 1401). 1931 is a big engineering year for us: the Empire State Building opened May 1 1931, the George
  Washington Bridge Oct 25 1931 and the Bayonne Bridge Nov 15 1931, so newsreels, newspaper and magazine photos and
  drawings published that year (opening day, the steelwork, the cables) become safe in the US for a January build.
  Only what was published in 1931 counts (an unpublished archive print follows other rules), and an archive's own
  terms of access still bind its copies. Not confirmed yet: a search at about 13:50 PT showed no 2027 list from Duke's
  Center for the Study of the Public Domain (its 2026 page is the model), and Wikipedia's "2027 in public domain"
  could not be read here (the fetch needed an approval nobody was there to give); Cord Cutters News' headline
  "Universal Horror Classics Frankenstein and Dracula Are Set to Enter Public Domain Soon" (search result, not read)
  points the same way (those monsters are not for us). For an Empire State build: ESRT Empire State Building, L.L.C.
  holds "EMPIRE STATE BUILDING" trademarks (Justia's list) and esbnyc.com has a "Licensing Application" page
  (titles seen in a search, pages not read): keep it editorial, as with the other landmarks.
  [17 U.S.C. § 304](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/17/304),
  [17 U.S.C. § 305](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/17/305),
  [17 U.S.C. § 1401](https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/17/1401),

## 2026-11-26 17:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

December dates (new to these notes: no Marconi and no Sydney Harbour Bridge line yet)
- Sat Dec 12, 125 years since the first radio signal across the Atlantic, a candidate build for that day (not in
  LINEUP, never built). IEEE's Milestone at Signal Hill, Newfoundland (dedicated Oct 4 1985): "At Signal Hill on
  December 12, 1901, Guglielmo Marconi and his assistant, George Kemp," "confirmed the reception of the first
  transatlantic radio signals." "With a telephone receiver and a wire antenna kept aloft by a kite," "they heard Morse
  code for the letter "S" transmitted from Poldhu, Cornwall." Its twin at Poldhu (dedicated Dec 12 2001; ETHW says the
  plaque went into storage, noted in 2015): sent "using equipment built by John Ambrose Fleming". "More than 2,000
  miles" (ETHW, History.com). Beats: a gale wrecked Poldhu's aerial on Sep 17 1901 (break); at Signal Hill the first
  kite was carried away by a gale and the second "trailed 500 feet of antenna wire" (fix; ETHW); the hook: the wave
  "had been headed into space" past the curve of the Earth, yet it "reflected off the ionosphere and bounced back down
  toward Canada" (History.com), a layer nobody knew of then: Kennelly and Heaviside next proposed "a layer of ionized
  air in the upper atmosphere" (ETHW). Our own sum for a stat line (check before use): over about 3,200 km the
  Earth's curve stands about 200 km above a straight line between the ends (d squared over 8R). Keep the script
  honest: "the only witness was George Kemp, hardly an impartial observer," and "the signals were too weak to operate
  an automatic recorder" (ETHW), so "Marconi heard", not "the world heard". Faces: Marconi and Kemp were real people,
  so AI shots show the kite, the huts and the masts with people only from behind; the 1901 kite photos show them
  (Bridgeman and Mary Evans sell those). Free proof-still lead, not read: Scientific American of Jan 4 1902 on Marconi
  (US public domain by date; a PDF copy at leradiodisophie.it). The 125th itself: nothing announced for Dec 2026 in
  what was read; the Lizard Wireless Station marked its own 125th in January 2026 (GB4LWS on air all month), and for
  the 120th the Marconi Radio Club of Newfoundland and the Poldhu Amateur Radio Club re-enacted the "s" at 1600 UTC
  on Dec 12 2021 from the Geo Centre on Signal Hill (ARRL): the real thing for a B5 if they do it again (look again
  in early December). As the 15:00 entry says, a December build that wants Seedance clips needs its queue pair before
  Nov 28; this one would otherwise get stills and the v2 look.
  [ETHW, Reception, 1901](https://ethw.org/Milestones%3AReception_of_Transatlantic_Radio_Signals%2C_1901),
  [ETHW, Transmission, 1901](https://ethw.org/Milestones%3ATransmission_of_Transatlantic_Radio_Signals%2C_1901),

- Wed Dec 23, 40 years since the Rutan Voyager landed from the first flight around the world with no stop and no
  refuel, a candidate build for that day (not in LINEUP, never built). It "took off from Edwards Air Force Base on
  December 14" and "carried more than three times its weight in fuel" (History.com); HistoryNet gives "7,011.5
  pounds" of fuel in a "9,694.5 pounds" takeoff weight on an airframe of "939 pounds" (our sum: fuel about 72 % of the
  takeoff weight). Beats read today: the wings "were so heavy with fuel that their tips scraped along the ground"
  (History.com) and "were soon shed", and it "lifted off 14,200 feet down the runway, a mere 800 feet from the end"
  (HistoryNet); built of "layers of carbon-fiber tape and paper impregnated with epoxy resin", wingspan "111 feet"
  (History.com; HistoryNet says 110, so say "about 110 feet"); it cruised "on the rear engine, to conserve fuel"
  (HistoryNet); "two tropical storms in the Pacific" (History.com) and a South Atlantic storm that "tossed Voyager
  like a cork" (HistoryNet); on Dec 23 "the engine it was using went out, and the aircraft plunged from 8,500 to 5,000
  feet" (History.com) until the other one "coughed to life" (HistoryNet); it landed at Edwards "after flying 25,012
  miles" (History.com; check it against the FAI record before a stat line) with "just five gallons of fuel left in
  its remaining operational fuel tank" (History.com; HistoryNet: "little more than 108 pounds (18 gallons)", about
  1.5 % of the fuel). The time differs by a minute ("nine days and four minutes", History.com; "nine days and three
  minutes", HistoryNet): say "just over nine days". It is "on permanent display at the National Air and Space Museum
  in Washington, D.C." (History.com). The two pilots were real people: the plane in every shot, people only from
  behind. Free proof still for B5 or the stat line: NASA's EC87-0029-02, "Voyager aircraft return from non-stop trip
  around the world" ("The Voyager aircraft circles before landing at Edwards Air Force Base", "December 23, 1986",

  (only a link to its usage guidelines), and DVIDS' copy (NIX-EC87-0029-02) is marked "PUBLIC DOMAIN" (its "Date

  opened, so look at it before use. Not free: HistoryNet's ("©Mark Greenberg", "AP Photo"); the Smithsonian's pages

## 2026-11-26 19:00 PT (hourly runner, cloud run)

Build 109, the Eastgate Centre in Harare (new to these notes: only its photo rule was here, Nov 26 12:10)
- The facts behind LINEUP 109, from the architect's own project page: "Commissioned March 1991", "Construction Jan
  1993 - April 1996", "Occupancy April 1996"; "Architects Pearce Partnership", "Engineers Ove Arup and Partners";
  "two buildings side by side linked together by a glass roof", "Total combined floor area 55000msq", "the seven
  floors of offices" (mickpearce.com). Arup: "When it first opened its doors in 1996, Eastgate was Zimbabwe's largest
  office and shopping development." How it cools, a chain for the shots: "32 banks of low and high volume fans draw
  air from the atrium through filters"; "the high volume fans run at night to give ten air changes per hour and low
  volume fans run during the day giving two air changes per hour"; the air is "fed through the hollow floors to low
  level grilles"; "The cold night air passing through the void festooned with concrete teeth removes the heat of the
  previous day"; the warm air leaves by "48 brick funnels topping internal stacks" (Pearce; ASME: "48 brick
  chimneys"), Arup's "stack effect" made "by allowing cool air to enter its base and warm air to discharge at roof
  level". So it runs fans: say "no ordinary air conditioning", never "no machines".
- The energy figure in LINEUP ("about a tenth") is Arup's "90% less energy to heat and cool", heating and cooling
  only. The architect's page says "Eastgate uses 35% less total energy than the average consumption of six other
  conventional buildings with full HVAC in Harare" and "less than 50% of the energy used in conventionally air
  conditioned buildings". Different baselines: a line names the one it uses, or takes a safer stat: "The saving on
  capital cost compared with full HVAC was 10% of total building cost" (Pearce; ASME: "10 percent less to build"),
  "48 CHIMNEYS", or "10 AIR CHANGES AN HOUR" by night. ASME (Mark Wolverton, Nov 21 2019) also has "saved $3.5
  million in energy costs during its first five years" with no source named: check before use. It is not cold
  inside: "3°C of cooling between outside and inside temperature is achieved", offices "up to 27 - 28°C on certain
  days", and "People and machines add 1.5°C to the internal temperature each day" (Pearce); ASME: "mid-70s degrees
  Fahrenheit", "may reach 80-82 degrees". So "cooler than the street", not "cold".
- A true twist for line 11 or the tag: the termite picture it copied is not how the mounds work. The old idea was the
  Swiss entomologist Martin Lüscher's ("many researchers believed the mounds acted as air conditioners"), made
  popular by Scientific American in 1961; Scott Turner found the mounds "didn't regulate temperature so much as push
  oxygen and carbon dioxide into and out of the nest", and "While the Eastgate Centre did work as planned, the mounds
  on which it was based functioned in an entirely different manner" (Nautilus, Lee Billings, Dec 13 2013). Harvard's
  team (L. Mahadevan, Hunter King, Samuel Ocko; field work in India; PNAS, 2015): the mounds "act similarly to a lung,
  inhaling and exhaling once a day as they are heated and cooled" (Harvard SEAS, Sep 10 2015). ASME quotes Guy
  Theraulaz: "Eastgate Centre was built on a wrong conception of how termites regulate the temperature of their
  nests." The line: it copied the termites wrong and still works (Nautilus quotes Turner: it "succeeded because Pearce
  was a very good architect rather than a crude imitator of nature"). What the two share is the day-night rhythm: the
  mound breathes once a day, and Eastgate's concrete soaks up the night's cool and gives it back by day. Pearce and
  Turner are real people: never shown; the building, mounds, fans, concrete and hands only.
  [Mick Pearce, Eastgate](https://mickpearce.com/Eastgate.html),
  [Arup, Eastgate](https://www.arup.com/projects/eastgate),
  [ASME, Nov 21 2019](https://www.asme.org/Topics-Resources/Content/What-Termites-Can-Teach-Engineers),
  [Nautilus, Dec 13 2013](https://nautil.us/the-termite-and-the-architect-234706/),
  [Harvard SEAS, Sep 10 2015](https://seas.harvard.edu/news/how-termites-ventilate)

  "the fastest launch", not "the fastest coaster", and the lost crown plus the new title is a ready twist. Why
  Falcons Flight's 250 km/h does not count as a launch is on none of the three pages: check before a line says it.
- For the shots (Wikipedia unless marked): a hydraulic launch "capable of 20,800 hp", 0-100 km/h in about two seconds
  over a 400 m launch run, up to 1.7 g in the launch and 4.8 g in the ride (GWR: 4.8), 2.07 km of track (GWR gives
  both 2.2 and 2.07), a 52 m hill, open since 4 Nov 2010, and "Riders are required to wear safety goggles to protect
  from airborne particles such as dust". How the train is braked is on none of the three pages.
  [GWR news, Apr 30 2026](https://www.guinnessworldrecords.com/news/2026/4/formula-rossa-achieves-highest-ever-rollercoaster-launch-speed-to-extend-record-legacy),
  [GWR record](https://www.guinnessworldrecords.com/world-records/780255-highest-speed-achieved-by-a-rollercoaster-launch),
  [Wikipedia, Formula Rossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_Rossa)

Build 114, the soda can: LINEUP's check sources read (new to these notes)
- CSMonitor (Apr 14 2015, on Bill Hammack's video): "less than a tenth of a millimeter thick"; "up to 90 pounds per
  square inch (about six times regular atmospheric pressure)". The car-tyre comparison is LINEUP's, not the page's
  (check a tyre figure before a line uses it), and 90 psi is the most a can holds, not its usual fill. The dome
  "allows the can to withstand greater pressures than if it were flat"; the top is "tapered in" for "aluminum
  savings" (Hackaday); full against empty is only the hand-crush comparison, no figure. A "flat disk a few inches
  across" is pressed into a shallow cup, then a taller one, in "a seventh of a second"; "about 100 billion cans each
  year" for the industry (the same page's "100 million cans in a sixth-month period" is one machine, likely a typo:
  keep the two apart); a coating inside against a metallic taste; carbon dioxide for soda, nitrogen for juice. On
  neither page: the can's weight, the lid's alloy, an inventor or a year.
  [CSMonitor, Apr 14 2015](https://csmonitor.com/Science/Science-Notebook/2015/0414/The-surprising-science-behind-the-aluminum-soda-can),
  [Hackaday, Apr 17 2015](https://hackaday.com/2015/04/17/you-betta-recognize-the-aluminum-beverage-can)

Free pictures (new to these notes)
- Lummi (lummi.ai), AI-made stock images: "All images are free for commercial and personal use without
  attribution", the one limit named "Cannot compile Lummi photos to create a competing stock photo service"
  (licenseorg's summary; Lummi's own licence was not read); stills only, no video. Look each one over for logo-like
  marks before use.
- rawpixel: Public Domain Review lists its museum and archive scans as "CC0 license", but its premium images have "a
  separate, more restrictive license" and licenseorg's own free-tier table marks commercial use as not allowed
  (against its text calling the free images "public domain or CC0"): so only an image whose own page says CC0 or
  public domain. rawpixel's own licence page is for a later run.
  [licenseorg, Lummi](https://licenseorg.com/guide/images/lummi),
  [Codrops, Jul 4 2024](https://tympanus.net/codrops/2024/07/04/lummi-say-goodbye-to-boring-stock-photos-forever),
  [licenseorg, rawpixel](https://licenseorg.com/guide/images/rawpixel),
  [Public Domain Review, rawpixel](https://publicdomainreview.org/collections/source/rawpixel/)

Checked, nothing new
